Geometric Eisenstein series I: Finiteness theorems
Linus Hamann, David Hansen and Peter Scholze

Abstract
. We develop the theory of geometric Eisenstein series and constant term functors for ℓ \ell -adic sheaves on stacks of bundles on the Fargues-Fontaine curve. In particular, we prove essentially optimal finiteness theorems for these functors, analogous to the usual finiteness properties of parabolic inductions and Jacquet modules. We also prove a geometric form of Bernstein’s second adjointness theorem, generalizing the classical result and its recent extension to more general coefficient rings proved in [ DHKM24 ] . As applications, we decompose the category of sheaves on Bun G \mathrm{Bun}_{G} into cuspidal and Eisenstein parts, and show that the gluing functors between strata of Bun G \mathrm{Bun}_{G} are continuous in a very strong sense.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Prologue

In
[
Far16
]
, Fargues laid out a remarkable and far-reaching vision, recasting many structures around the representation theory of
p
-adic groups and the local Langlands correspondence as
geometric
Langlands over the Fargues-Fontaine curve. The work of Fargues-Scholze
[
FS21
]
then put this vision on solid technical foundations and applied it to construct a general semisimple local Langlands correspondence.

In this paper and its sequel
[
HHS
]
, we develop some additional foundations for this program. More precisely, our goal in these papers is to geometrize the classical operations of parabolic induction and Jacquet module into functors between sheaf categories on suitable moduli stacks. In the present paper we define these functors and prove their basic finiteness properties.

1.2.
Main results

To state our main results, let
E
be a non-archimedean local field, and let
H/E
be a linear algebraic group. One then has the associated Artin v-stack
\mathrm{Bun}_{H}
parametrizing
H
-bundles on the Fargues-Fontaine curve. This is functorial in
H
. In particular, if
G
is a connected reductive group and
P=MU\subset G
is a parabolic subgroup, we have a natural diagram

\mathrm{Bun}_{M}\xleftarrow{\mathfrak{q}}\mathrm{Bun}_{P}\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{p}}\mathrm{Bun}_{G}.

We note that
\mathfrak{q}
is cohomologically smooth, but not representable, while
\mathfrak{p}
is representable in locally spatial diamonds but is neither smooth nor proper.

Now, let
\Lambda
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}[\sqrt{q}]
-algebra, where
q
denotes the cardinality of the residue field of
E
and
\ell\nmid q
. One can define a canonical invertible object
\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}}\in D(\mathrm{Bun}_{P},\Lambda)
, which is a square root of the dualizing complex and can be described explicitly in terms of the modulus character
[
HI23
, Theorem 1.5]
. Our main interest is in the functors
1
1

1

At present, the six-functor formalism required to make sense of these formulas is only developed in the literature under the additional assumption that
\Lambda
is killed by a power of
\ell
. However, forthcoming work
[
Sch
]
of the third author will correct this issue. For the moment, the reader may wish to assume that
\Lambda
is a torsion ring. However, we have written this paper in such a way that once the formalism developed in
[
Sch
]
is available, everything will apply to general
\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}[\sqrt{q}]
-algebras.

\displaystyle\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}(-)

\displaystyle:=\mathfrak{p}_{!}(\mathfrak{q}^{\ast}(-)\otimes\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}}),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Eis}_{P\ast}(-)

\displaystyle:=\mathfrak{p}_{\ast}(\mathfrak{q}^{\ast}(-)\otimes\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}}),

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{CT}_{P*}(-)

\displaystyle:=\mathfrak{q}_{\ast}(\mathfrak{p}^{!}(-)\otimes\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}}^{-1}),

\displaystyle\mathrm{CT}_{P!}(-)

\displaystyle:=\mathfrak{q}_{!}(\mathfrak{p}^{*}(-)\otimes\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}}).

The first two functors here are two possible geometrizations of normalized parabolic induction: Over the locus of trivial
G
-bundles, the map
\mathfrak{p}
is proper and so both functors agree there. The functor
\mathrm{CT}_{P!}
is the natural geometrization of the normalized Jacquet module and is the left adjoint of
\mathrm{Eis}_{P*}
via the identification
\mathfrak{q}^{*}(-)\otimes\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}}^{\otimes 2}=\mathfrak{q}^{!}(-)
. On the other hand,
\mathrm{CT}_{P*}
is the right adjoint of
\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}
by definition. By the “second adjunction” theorem of Bernstein, this happens to agree with the normalized Jacquet module for the opposite parabolic in classical representation theory; one of our main theorems will be an extension of second adjunction to our geometric setting.

In the classical world of representation theory, parabolic inductions and Jacquet modules have extremely good finiteness properties. With characteristic zero coefficients these are classical results of Bernstein, and with general coefficients these are very recent results of Dat-Helm-Kurinczuk-Moss
[
DHKM24
]
(incidentally, relying on
[
FS21
]
). Informally, our main result says that the geometrizations of these functors still have very strong finiteness properties.

More precisely, recall that the sheaf category
D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)
constructed in
[
FS21
]
comes with two intrinsic finiteness conditions, namely the notions of compact sheaves and ULA sheaves. Moreover, there are two natural duality operations on
D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)
, Verdier duality, denoted
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{Verd}}
, and Bernstein-Zelevinsky duality, denoted
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}
, which induce self-anti-equivalences on ULA sheaves and compact sheaves, respectively. Our main results explain how these finiteness conditions and their dualities interact with the functors of Eisenstein series and constant term.

Theorem 1.2.1

(Theorem
3.1.2
, Corollary
3.1.3
, Theorem
3.2.1
, Theorem
7.1.1
)
.

Let
G\supset P=MU
be as above, with
P^{-}
the opposite parabolic.

(1)

The functor
\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}
preserves compact objects. If
A\in D(\mathrm{Bun}_{M},\Lambda)
is ULA and supported on finitely many connected components, then
\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}(A)
and
\mathrm{Eis}_{P\ast}(A)
are ULA.

(2)

The functor
\mathrm{CT}_{P*}
preserves ULA objects. If
A\in D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)
is compact, then
\mathrm{CT}_{P!}(A)|_{\mathrm{Bun}_{M}^{\alpha}}
is compact for all
\alpha\in\pi_{0}(\mathrm{Bun}_{M})
.

(3)

There is a canonical isomorphism of functors
\mathrm{CT}_{P!}\cong\mathrm{CT}_{P^{-}\ast}
.

(4)

We have the following duality isomorphisms:

i.
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}\cong\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{-}!}\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}^{M}
on compact objects.

ii.
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{Verd}}\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}\cong\mathrm{Eis}_{P\ast}\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{Verd}}^{M}
on all objects.

iii.
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{Verd}}^{M}\mathrm{CT}_{P!}\cong\mathrm{CT}_{P\ast}\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{Verd}}
on all objects.

We will also show that (3) is logically equivalent to the duality isomorphism (4).i. These statements can both be regarded as geometrizations of Bernstein’s famous second adjointness theorem.

We now sketch some of the ideas in the proofs. The duality isomorphisms (4).ii-(4).iii are straightforward consequences of the definitions, and we state them for completeness. The first serious task is to prove that
\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}
preserves compact objects. We prove this by a complicated but reasonably direct induction, building on the fact that after restricting to certain strata in
\mathrm{Bun}_{M}
,
\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}
is closely related to classical parabolic inductions and to the gluing functors
i_{b\sharp}
defined in
[
FS21
]
, both of which are known to preserve compact objects. Note that the work of Dat-Helm-Kurinczuk-Moss
[
DHKM24
]
is a key input to this induction, and we do not reprove their results. Once we know that
\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}
preserves compact objects, it is formal to see that
\mathrm{CT}_{P\ast}
preserves ULA objects. With this preservation of compacts in hand, we then define a canonical map
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}\to\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{-}!}\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}^{M}
. We prove this map is an isomorphism by an inductive argument parallel to the proof that
\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}
preserves compact objects, using classical second adjunction as well as a similar adjunction for the gluing functor
i_{b\sharp}
as input.
2
2

2

While we were finalizing this paper, these results have been proved independently by Takaya
[
Tak24
]
, along very similar lines.

The remaining results in (1)-(2) lie deeper, and require some new geometric input. The key idea here is that
\mathrm{Bun}_{P}
admits a relative compactification over
\mathrm{Bun}_{G}
which is still well-behaved relative to
\mathrm{Bun}_{M}
. This is the famous
Drinfeld compactification

\widetilde{\mathrm{Bun}}_{P}
, which is a fundamental object in the classical geometric Langlands program. Roughly speaking, this stack parametrizes
G
-bundles equipped with a generic reduction to a
P
-bundle; see Definition
4.2.1
and §
6.3
for the precise definition. However, we emphasize that while it is reasonably straightforward to copy the definition of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Bun}}_{P}
into the setting of
[
FS21
]
, its desired geometric properties are not obvious in this setting.

Theorem 1.2.2

(Proposition
4.2.3
, Theorem
4.2.4
, Proposition
4.2.12
, Theorem
6.2.1
, Proposition
6.3.3
)
.

Let
G\supset P=MU
be as above.

(1)

There is a naturally defined Artin v-stack
\widetilde{\mathrm{Bun}}_{P}
sitting in a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{p}_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{j}_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{q}_{P}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathrm{Bun}}_{P}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{P}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Bun}_{G}}
\textstyle{\text{}\mathrm{Bun}_{M}}

where
\tilde{j}_{P}
is an open immersion. The map
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{P}
is partially proper and representable in locally spatial diamonds, and of locally finite
\mathrm{dim.trg}
.

(2)

If
\mathrm{Bun}_{M}^{\alpha}\subset\mathrm{Bun}_{M}
is any connected component with open-closed preimage
\widetilde{\mathrm{Bun}}_{P}^{\alpha}
, the induced map
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{P}^{\alpha}:\widetilde{\mathrm{Bun}}_{P}^{\alpha}\to\mathrm{Bun}_{G}
is proper.

(3)

The stack
\widetilde{\mathrm{Bun}}_{P}
admits a locally closed stratification by certain strata
\phantom{}{}_{\theta}\widetilde{\mathrm{Bun}}_{P}
, indexed by
\theta\in\Lambda_{G,P}^{\mathrm{pos}}
. Each stratum has a canonical fiber product decomposition

\phantom{}{}_{\theta}\widetilde{\mathrm{Bun}}_{P}\cong\mathrm{Bun}_{P}\times_{\mathrm{Bun}_{M}}\mathrm{Mod}^{+,\theta}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{M}},

where
\mathrm{Mod}^{+,\theta}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{M}}
is a certain closed subspace of a relative Beilinson-Drinfeld Grassmannian living over
\mathrm{Div}^{(\theta)}
, a partially symmetrized version of the mirror curve.

(4)

The sheaves
\tilde{j}_{P!}(\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}})
and
\tilde{j}_{P\ast}(\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}})
are
\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}_{P}
-ULA.

The proofs of (1)-(3) require a good understanding of relatively flat coherent sheaves on relative Fargues-Fontaine curves. This theory was initiated in
[
ALB21
]
, further refined in
[
Ham22
, Section 5.2.3]
, and we now develop it further here. In particular, given a relative Fargues-Fontaine curve
X_{S}
equipped with a relatively flat coherent sheaf
\mathcal{E}/X_{S}
, we make a careful study of the moduli space
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{ub}_{\mathcal{E}}^{n,d}
parametrizing fiberwise-injective maps
\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{E}
from a (variable) relatively flat coherent sheaf
\mathcal{F}
of fiberwise-constant generic rank
n
and degree
d
. The first key result is that
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{ub}_{\mathcal{E}}^{n,d}
is a locally spatial diamond which is partially proper and locally of finite dim.trg over
S
, and the subfunctor where the quotient sheaf
\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}
is fiberwise a vector bundle is open. This formally implies (1), and related arguments reduce the properness in (2) to the following theorem.

Theorem 1.2.3
.

The natural map
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{ub}_{\mathcal{E}}^{n,d}\to S
is proper.

When
\mathcal{E}
is a vector bundle and
n=1
, this follows easily from results in
[
FS21
]
. In the general case, one can easily reduce to the case that
\mathcal{E}
is a vector bundle. Then the statement is equivalent to the assertion that any given set of sub-vector bundles
\mathcal{F}_{i}\subset\mathcal{E}\times_{X_{S}}X_{C_{i}}
(defined over fields
\mathrm{Spa}C_{i}
) of constant rank and degree induce a sub-vector bundle
\mathcal{F}
of
\mathcal{E}\times_{X_{S}}X_{T}
for the “product of points”
T=\mathrm{Spa}(\prod_{i}\mathcal{O}_{C_{i}})[\tfrac{1}{\varpi}]
. Producing this sub-vector bundle is a question that is local on the Fargues-Fontaine curve, but the assumption of constant rank and degree is a (necessary) global condition that we did not see how to make use of. Eventually, we realized the (in hindsight obvious) possibility of using the determinant map. This reduces one to proving properness of the map

\bigwedge^{n}:\mathcal{S}\mathrm{ub}_{\mathcal{E}}^{n,d}\to\mathcal{S}\mathrm{ub}_{\bigwedge^{n}\mathcal{E}}^{1,d}

where now one can in fact forget the degree restriction. After this reduction, it is possible to localize the statement from the Fargues-Fontaine curve
X_{S}
to its cover
Y_{S}
or some affinoid piece thereof, and argue by explicit commutative algebra.

With the geometry under control, it remains to prove Theorem
1.2.2
.(4). The analogue of this result in classical geometric Langlands is well-known
[
BG02
, Theorem 5.1.5]
, but the standard proof makes use of Deligne’s generic ULA theorem to ensure the existence of some open subspace in
\mathrm{Bun}_{M}
over which the ULA result holds, which can then be spread out to all of
\mathrm{Bun}_{M}
by Hecke correspondences. Since the generic ULA theorem is definitely false in
p
-adic geometry, this approach is not available to us. Another approach for the Borel is provided in
[
BG02
, Section 5.2]
; however, this argument fails in our situation at the very first step, since being ULA over the point is not a trivial condition in the setting of adic geometry and the sheaf categories we are considering. To solve our problems, we instead make a careful induction on the boundary strata of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Bun}}_{P}
, using hyperbolic localization to propagate the ULA property deeper and deeper into the boundary. The key idea here is embodied in the following result, which works in essentially any six-functor formalism.

Proposition 1.2.4
.

Let
\pi:X\to S
be a reasonable map equipped with a section
i:S\to X
and a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action which is totally attracting towards the section. Let
X^{\circ}=X\smallsetminus S
with
j:X^{\circ}\to X
the natural open immersion, and set
\pi^{\circ}=\pi\circ j
. If
A\in D(X^{\circ},\Lambda)
is
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant and
\pi^{\circ}
-ULA, then
j_{!}A
is
\pi
-ULA.

Note that as a formal consequence, we get that also
j_{\ast}A
is
\pi
-ULA, and that
i^{\ast}j_{\ast}A\in D(S,\Lambda)
is dualizable. We also see that if
B\in D(X,\Lambda)
is any
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant sheaf, then it is
\pi
-ULA iff
j^{\ast}B
is
\pi^{\circ}
-ULA and
i^{\ast}B
is dualizable.

To employ this technique, we need good local models for the boundary strata of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Bun}}_{P}
. These are provided by the theory of
Zastava spaces
, which smooth-locally model suitable portions of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Bun}}_{P}
in terms of certain intersections of semi-infinite orbits inside the
B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}
-affine Grassmannians. For all
\theta\in\Lambda_{G,P}^{\mathrm{pos}}
, there exists a Zastava space
\tilde{Z}^{\theta}\rightarrow\mathrm{Mod}^{+,\theta}_{M}
, equipped with a section
\mathfrak{s}^{\theta}:\mathrm{Mod}^{+,\theta}_{M}\rightarrow\tilde{Z}^{\theta}
and (étale locally on
\mathrm{Mod}^{+,\theta}_{M}
) a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action which attracts to the section. The image of this section is smooth-locally isomorphic to the strata
\phantom{}{}_{\theta}\widetilde{\mathrm{Bun}}_{P}
, which puts us in a situation where we can apply the previous Theorem (or rather a slight generalization of it, see Theorem
6.1.3
for the exact statement we need) to inductively propagate the ULA property of the sheaf
\tilde{j}_{P!}(\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}})
into deeper and deeper strata of the Drinfeld compactification.

With Theorem
1.2.2
in hand, the remaining statements in Theorem
1.2.1
.(1)-(2) follow from some straightforward manipulations with the definitions of the functors, using the miraculous properties of ULA sheaves quite heavily; see Theorem
7.1.1
for details.

We also note that our argument for Theorem
1.2.2
.(4) goes through without change in the setting of classical geometric Langlands, and gives a new proof which works uniformly in all the sheaf theories usually considered in that setting.

1.3.
Some applications

Our results have immediate applications to the gluing functors between strata in
\mathrm{Bun}_{G}
. More precisely, recall that, for any
b\in B(G)
, we have functors

i_{b\sharp},i_{b!},i_{b\ast}:D(G_{b}(E),\Lambda)\to D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)

and also functors

i_{b}^{\ast},i_{b}^{!}:D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)\to D(G_{b}(E),\Lambda).

These satisfy adjunctions

i_{b\sharp}\dashv i_{b}^{\ast}\dashv i_{b\ast}\;\;\mathrm{and}\;\;i_{b!}\dashv i_{b}^{!}.

Moreover, the analysis in
[
FS21
]
shows that
i_{b\sharp},i_{b!},
and
i_{b}^{\ast}
preserve compact objects, while
i_{b!}
,
i_{b\ast}
,
i_{b}^{\ast}
and
i_{b}^{!}
preserve ULA objects. The following result settles the remaining finiteness questions for these functors.

Theorem 1.3.1
.

The functor
i_{b\sharp}
preserves ULA objects, and the functor
i_{b}^{!}
preserves compact objects. If
A\in D(G_{b}(E),\Lambda)
is compact, then
i_{b\ast}A
has compact stalks.

As another application, with rational coefficients, we decompose the category
D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)
into cuspidal and Eisenstein parts. More precisely, assume that
G
is quasisplit, and define
D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)^{\mathrm{Eis}}
as the full subcategory generated under colimits by the images of the functors
\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}
for all proper parabolics
P
, and define
D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)^{\mathrm{cusp}}
as the common kernel of the functors
\mathrm{CT}_{P\ast}
for all proper parabolics
P
.

Theorem 1.3.2
.

(1)

The subcategory
D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)^{\mathrm{cusp}}
is stable under all limits and colimits, and under Verdier duality. Any object
A\in D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)^{\mathrm{cusp}}
is both
!
- and
\ast
-extended from the semistable locus
\mathrm{Bun}_{G}^{\mathrm{ss}}\subset\mathrm{Bun}_{G}
, and
i_{b}^{\ast}A\in D(G_{b}(E),\Lambda)
is cuspidal in the sense of Vigneras (i.e all of its Jacquet modules with respect to proper parabolics are
0
) for any basic
b
.

(2)

The pair
D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)^{\mathrm{Eis}},D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)^{\mathrm{cusp}}
forms a semiorthogonal decomposition of
D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)
.

(3)

When
\Lambda=\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}}
, there is a canonical
orthogonal
decomposition

D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}})=D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}})^{\mathrm{Eis}}\oplus D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}})^{\mathrm{cusp}}

stable under Verdier duality, and (on compact objects) under Bernstein-Zelevinsky duality.

In the proof of Theorem
1.3.2
.(3), we use the theory of Bernstein components rather critically, which restricts us to characteristic zero coefficients. In fact, via reduction modulo
\ell
, cuspidal Bernstein components can meet with Eisenstein components, so it is necessary to invert
\ell
in part (3).

Note that in classical geometric Langlands, one only has a semiorthogonal decomposition: an orthogonal decomposition as in (3) cannot hold, since the locus of irreducible local systems is dense in the stack of
L
-parameters. In our setting, however, the irreducible locus (i.e the locus defined by unramified twists of supercuspidal parameters) is open and closed in the stack of
L
-parameters
\mathrm{Par}_{G}
. In fact, writing
e^{\mathrm{irr}},e^{\mathrm{red}}\in\mathcal{O}(\mathrm{Par}_{G})
for the complementary idempotents cutting out the irreducible and reducible loci, the spectral action gives a similar decomposition

D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}})\cong e^{\mathrm{red}}D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}})\oplus e^{\mathrm{irr}}D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}})

and some mild results on the compatibility of Eisenstein functors with
L
-parameters (that follow easily from our inductive arguments analyzing the Eisenstein functor in terms of parabolic induction and the gluing functor
i_{b\sharp}
) imply that

D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}})^{\mathrm{Eis}}\subset e^{\mathrm{red}}D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}})

and hence dually

D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}})^{\mathrm{cusp}}\supset e^{\mathrm{irr}}D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}})

We (and also Takaya
[
Tak24
]
) conjecture that these inclusions are equalities.

1.4.
Other applications and future developments

We end the introduction by commenting on the sequel paper
[
HHS
]
and some other works which rely on the results in this paper.

First, we note that some of the main results in the paper
[
Ham22
]
, which were applied in
[
HL23
]
to prove vanishing theorems for the cohomology of
global
Shimura varieties, crucially relied on Theorem
1.2.2
.(4) in the case where
P=B
is a Borel.
3
3

3

This case does not seem substantially easier than the case of a general parabolic.
Thus the present paper makes
[
Ham22
]
and
[
HL23
]
entirely unconditional.

In the follow-up paper
[
HHS
]
, we will establish the compatibility of Eisenstein and constant term functors with Hecke operators, and give applications to the cohomology of local Shimura varieties. We also expect to construct and analyze the intersection cohomology sheaf
\mathrm{IC}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{Bun}}_{P}}
on
\widetilde{\mathrm{Bun}}_{P}
and the associated compactified Eisenstein series functor
\mathrm{Eis}_{P!\ast}
. Since there is no general theory of perverse sheaves in
p
-adic geometry, the sheaf
\mathrm{IC}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{Bun}}_{P}}
does not exist for soft reasons. We eventually realized that it is possible to construct it directly by adapting some ideas from
[
Gai22
]
.
4
4

4

We thank Joakim Faergeman and Andreas Hayash for patient explanations about these matters.
This construction requires us to import yet more techniques from geometric Langlands - in particular, ideas around Ran geometry and factorization structures - into the Fargues-Scholze setting.

Remarkably, the categorical geometric Langlands conjecture has a natural adaptation to the Fargues-Scholze setting, where it translates into a categorical upgrade of the local Langlands conjecture (see
[
FS21
, Conjecture I.10.2]
for a precise statement). In ongoing work
[
HM
]
, the second author and Lucas Mann have formulated a program to prove this conjecture with
\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}}
-coefficients for many groups, in particular for
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
. Since the cuspidal part of the categorical conjecture is known for many groups (including
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
) by global methods, the decomposition of Theorem
1.3.2
.(3) allows one to focus on the Eisenstein part, where the problem clearly has a flavor which is inductive on Levis. To deal with the Eisenstein part, a key ingredient in the strategy of
[
HM
]
is the a priori compatibility of a certain functor from the automorphic side to the spectral side with
\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}
and its spectral analogue. This compatibility will be addressed elsewhere, building on the techniques in this paper and
[
HHS
]
.

Notation

Throughout this paper,
E
denotes a fixed nonarchimedean local field, with ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{E}
, uniformizer
\pi
and residue field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. We fix a separable closure
\overline{E}
with completion
C
, and let
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
be its residue field. We denote the absolute Galois group by
\Gamma=\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{E}/E)
. As usual, we denote by
\breve{E}\subset C
the completion of the maximal unramified extension of
E
, with residue field
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
. We write
\sigma
for the automorphism of
\breve{E}
lifting the
q
-th power Frobenius.

For coefficients, we fix a
\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}[\sqrt{q}]
-algebra
\Lambda
. Using the fixed choice of
\sqrt{q}\in\Lambda
, we can form distinguished square roots of modulus characters, etc. As our sheaf theory, we will be using the base change of the motivic
6
-functor formalism from
[
Sch
]
along the symmetric monoidal functor
D_{\mathrm{et,mot}}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q})\to D(\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})\to D(\Lambda)
given by the étale realization. If some power of
\ell
vanishes in
\Lambda
, this agrees with
D_{\mathrm{et}}
considered in
[
FS21
]
. In general, when specialized to
\mathrm{Bun}_{G}
, it agrees with the hand-crafted
D_{\mathrm{lis}}
considered in
[
FS21
]
. Contrary to the
D_{\mathrm{lis}}
-theory, the motivic formalism satisfies excision in general, which we will excessively use.

Throughout, we fix a reductive group
G
over
E
. As a rule, we do not make any further assumptions on
G
. At certain points, it is however convenient to assume that
G
is quasisplit, in which case we usually fix a choice of maximal torus and Borel
T\subset B\subset G
.

As usual,
B(G)=G(\breve{E})/(g\sim hg\sigma(h)^{-1})
denotes the Kottwitz set of
G
. For
b\in B(G)
(or rather a representative in
G(\breve{E})
) we write
G_{b}
for the
\sigma
-centralizer of
b
. We recall that the Kottwitz set
B(G)
is equipped with two maps:

(1)

The slope homomorphism

\nu:B(G)\rightarrow\mathbb{X}_{*}(T_{\overline{E}})^{+,\Gamma}_{\mathbb{Q}}

b\mapsto\nu_{b},

where
\mathbb{X}_{*}(T_{\overline{E}})_{\mathbb{Q}}^{+}
is the set of rational geometric dominant cocharacters of a maximal torus
T_{\overline{E}}\subset B_{\overline{E}}\subset G_{\overline{E}}
.

(2)

The Kottwitz invariant

\kappa_{G}:B(G)\rightarrow\pi_{1}(G)_{\Gamma},

where
\pi_{1}(G)=\pi_{1}(G_{\overline{E}})
denotes the algebraic fundamental group of Borovoi.

We write
B(G)_{\mathrm{basic}}\subset B(G)
for the set of basic elements; i.e. the elements for which
\nu_{b}
factors through the center of
G
. We follow the standard normalization of these invariants so that for
G=\mathbb{G}_{m}
, the element
b=\pi\in\breve{E}^{\times}
maps (under both maps) to
1\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{G}_{m})=\mathbb{Z}
. If we identify
B(G)=|\mathrm{Bun}_{G}|
, this means, however, that
\nu(\mathcal{O}(1))=\kappa(\mathcal{O}(1))=-1
, noting that the line bundle
\mathcal{O}(1)
corresponds to the
\sigma
-conjugacy class of
\pi^{-1}
. Thus, in bundle-theoretic terms,
-\nu
gives the usual slopes.

By
\mathrm{Bun}_{G}
, we denote the stack of
G
-bundles, taking any perfectoid space
S
to the groupoid of
G
-bundles on the Fargues-Fontaine curve
X_{S}
. We note that we always work over the fixed base
\ast:=\mathop{\rm Spd}{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}
, and denote by
\mathrm{Perf}
the category of perfectoid spaces in characteristic
p
over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
endowed with the
v
-topology. For
b\in B(G)
, we write
\mathcal{F}_{b}
for the associated
G
-bundle on
X_{S}
, for any
S\in\mathrm{Perf}
. We write
i_{b}:\mathrm{Bun}_{G}^{b}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Bun}_{G}
for the locally closed Harder-Narasimhan-stratum on it.

As usual, we denote by
\mathrm{Div}^{1}:=\mathop{\rm Spd}{\breve{E}}/\mathrm{Frob}^{\mathbb{Z}}
the mirror curve, and, for a finite extension
E^{\prime}/E
, we write
\mathrm{Div}^{1}_{E^{\prime}}
for the corresponding object for
E^{\prime}
. More generally, for
I
a finite index set, we let
\mathrm{Div}^{I}
denote the product
\prod_{i\in I}\mathrm{Div}^{1}
. For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we let
\mathrm{Div}^{(n)}=(\mathrm{Div}^{1})^{n}/S_{n}
denote the
n
th symmetric power of the mirror curve, where
S_{n}
is the symmetric group on
n
letters.

We consider parabolics
P\subset G
with Levi factor
M
and unipotent radical
U
. Unless we also use an opposite parabolic
P^{-}
, we treat
M
merely as a quotient of
P
and do not fix an embedding
M\subset P
. We write
\tilde{\mathcal{J}}_{G,P}
for the set of fundamental coroots corresponding to
P\subset G
; this is a
\Gamma
-invariant finite set of cocharacters
\mathbb{G}_{m}\to Z(M)_{\overline{E}}
. We let
\mathcal{J}_{G,P}=\tilde{\mathcal{J}}_{G,P}/\Gamma
be the set of Galois orbits.

We write
M^{\mathrm{ab}}
for the abelianization of
M
, and let
\Lambda_{G,P}:=\mathbb{X}_{*}(M^{\mathrm{ab}}_{\overline{E}})_{\Gamma}=B(M^{\mathrm{ab}})
denote the coinvariant lattice. The coroots define a canonical injective map

\mathcal{J}_{G,P}=\tilde{\mathcal{J}}_{G,P}/\Gamma\to\mathbb{X}_{*}(M^{\mathrm{ab}}_{\overline{E}})_{\Gamma}=\Lambda_{G,P}:i\mapsto\alpha_{i}

and we let
\Lambda_{G,P}^{\mathrm{pos}}\subset\Lambda_{G,P}
be the free abelian monoid generated by
\mathcal{J}_{G,P}
.

The adjoint action of
P
on
\mathrm{Lie}(U)
induces

P\to\mathrm{Aut}(\det\mathrm{Lie}(U))\cong\mathbb{G}_{m},

which factors through
M^{\mathrm{ab}}
and defines an element which we denote by
\xi_{P}\in\mathbb{X}^{*}(M^{\mathrm{ab}})
.

2.
The geometry of
\mathrm{Bun}_{P}
and Eisenstein functors

We will now review the geometry of
\mathrm{Bun}_{P}
and the description of the geometric Eisenstein functors following
[
HI23
]
.

2.1.
Eisenstein functors and the geometry of
\mathrm{Bun}_{P}

Let
E
be a nonarchimedean local field, let
G
be a reductive group over
E
, and let
P\subset G
be a parabolic with Levi quotient
M
. Let
M^{\mathrm{ab}}
be its torus quotient. We recall that we have a natural diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Bun}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Bun}_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Bun}_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Bun}_{M^{\mathrm{ab}}}}
.
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{p}_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{q}_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{q}^{\dagger}_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{M}}

By
[
FS21
, Corollary IV.1.23]
, we have decompositions

\mathrm{Bun}_{M}=\bigsqcup_{\alpha\in\pi_{1}(M)_{\Gamma}}\mathrm{Bun}_{M}^{\alpha}\text{ and
}\mathrm{Bun}_{M^{\mathrm{ab}}}=\bigsqcup_{\overline{\alpha}\in\mathbb{X}_{*}(M^{\mathrm{ab}}_{\overline{E}})_{\Gamma}}\mathrm{Bun}_{M^{\mathrm{ab}}}^{\overline{\alpha}}

into connected components via the Kottwitz invariant. We let
\mathrm{Bun}_{P}^{\alpha}
denote the fiber of
\mathfrak{q}_{P}
over the connected component
\mathrm{Bun}_{M}^{\alpha}
, and write
\mathfrak{q}_{P}^{\alpha}
(resp.
\mathfrak{p}_{P}^{\alpha}
) for the base-change of
\mathfrak{q}_{P}
(resp.
\mathfrak{p}_{P}
) to this connected component. We write
\overline{(-)}
for the natural composition:

\pi_{1}(M)_{\Gamma}\xrightarrow{\simeq}^{\kappa_{M}^{-1}}B(M)_{\mathrm{basic}}\rightarrow B(M^{\mathrm{ab}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}^{\kappa_{M^{\mathrm{ab}}}}\mathbb{X}_{*}(M^{\mathrm{ab}}_{\overline{E}})_{\Gamma}.

This map is an isomorphism when the derived group of
M
is simply connected, and in general is surjective with finite fibers. In Section
6.3
, we will explain a general reduction to the case that the derived group of
G
(hence, that of
M
) is simply connected, so the reader is invited to make this extra assumption.

Remark 2.1.1
.

The notation
\mathrm{Bun}_{M}^{\alpha}
for the connected component attached to
\alpha\in\pi_{1}(M)_{\Gamma}\xrightarrow{\simeq}^{\kappa_{M}^{-1}}B(M)_{\mathrm{basic}}
is perhaps a bit confusing, since it could also refer to the HN-strata attached to the element
\alpha
. To alleviate the confusion, we will always write
b_{\alpha}\in B(M)_{\mathrm{basic}}
for the element attached to
\alpha\in\pi_{1}(M)_{\Gamma}
under the isomorphism
\kappa_{M}^{-1}
, and in turn always write
\mathrm{Bun}_{M}^{b_{\alpha}}
for the HN-strata.

We recall the following result.

Proposition 2.1.2
.

[
HI23
, Proposition 3.5]

The map
\mathfrak{q}_{P}
is a cohomologically smooth map of Artin
v
-stacks with connected fibers. Over the connected component
\mathrm{Bun}_{M}^{\alpha}
it is pure of
\ell
-dimension equal to
d_{\alpha}:=\langle\xi_{P},\overline{\alpha}\rangle
.

This gives the following corollary, using that cohomologically smooth maps are universally open.

Corollary 2.1.3
.

We have a decomposition into connected components:

\mathrm{Bun}_{P}=\bigsqcup_{\alpha\in B(M)_{\mathrm{basic}}}\mathrm{Bun}_{P}^{\alpha}.

We recall that
\mathrm{Bun}_{M}
is cohomologically smooth of pure
\ell
-dimension
0
by
[
FS21
, Theorem 1.4.1]
. It therefore follows from the previous corollary that
\mathrm{Bun}_{P}
is cohomologically smooth. We write

\dim(\mathrm{Bun}_{P}):|\mathrm{Bun}_{P}|\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}

for the locally constant function specified by the
\ell
-dimension. The previous Proposition tells us that this is equal to
d_{\alpha}
on the connected component
\mathrm{Bun}_{P}^{\alpha}
. However, the dualizing complex on
\mathrm{Bun}_{P}
also includes some character twists which one needs to incorporate in order to get the morally correct “normalized” Eisenstein functor.

Definition 2.1.4
.

Let
\delta_{P}=|\xi_{P}|:M(E)\rightarrow\Lambda^{*}
(resp.
\delta_{P}^{1/2}=|\xi_{P}|^{\tfrac{1}{2}}
) denote the modulus character of
P
(resp. square root of the modulus character of
P
, fixed by our choice of
\sqrt{q}\in\Lambda
). As
\xi_{P}
factors over
M^{\mathrm{ab}}
, these factor over characters
\overline{\delta}_{P}
(resp.
\overline{\delta}_{P}^{1/2}
) of
M^{\mathrm{ab}}(E)
. We let
\overline{\Delta}_{P}\in D(\mathrm{Bun}_{M^{\mathrm{ab}}},\Lambda)
(resp.
\overline{\Delta}_{P}^{1/2}
), be the sheaf whose value on the connected component
\mathrm{Bun}_{M^{\mathrm{ab}}}^{\overline{\alpha}}\simeq[\ast/\underline{M^{\mathrm{ab}}(E)}]
is given by the smooth characters
\overline{\delta}_{P}
(resp.
\overline{\delta}_{P}^{1/2}
) for all
\overline{\alpha}\in\mathbb{X}_{*}(M^{\mathrm{ab}}_{\overline{E}})_{\Gamma}
. We write
\Delta_{P}
(resp.
\Delta_{P}^{1/2}
) for
q_{M}^{*}(\overline{\Delta}_{P})
(resp.
q_{M}^{*}(\overline{\Delta}_{P}^{1/2})
), and set
\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}}:=\mathfrak{q}_{P}^{*}(\Delta_{P}^{1/2})[\dim(\mathrm{Bun}_{P})]
.

The key result is then as follows.

Theorem 2.1.5
.

[
HI23
]

There is an isomorphism

K_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}}\simeq\mathfrak{q}_{P}^{*}(\Delta_{P})[2\dim(\mathrm{Bun}_{P})]

of sheaves in
D(\mathrm{Bun}_{P},\Lambda)
. In particular, the sheaf
\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}}
is Verdier self-dual.

We recall that we have an isomorphism
K_{\mathrm{Bun}_{M}}\simeq\Lambda

[
HI23
, Proposition 1.1]
. Using this, we deduce the following corollary.

Corollary 2.1.6
.

We have an isomorphism

K_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}/\mathrm{Bun}_{M}}\simeq\mathfrak{q}_{P}^{*}(\Delta_{P})[2\dim(\mathrm{Bun}_{P})]

of sheaves in
D(\mathrm{Bun}_{P},\Lambda)
.

Definition 2.1.7
.

We define the normalized Eisenstein functors

\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}:D(\mathrm{Bun}_{M},\Lambda)\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)

A\mapsto\mathfrak{p}_{P!}(\mathfrak{q}_{P}^{*}(A)\otimes\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}})

and

\mathrm{Eis}_{P*}:D(\mathrm{Bun}_{M},\Lambda)\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)

A\mapsto\mathfrak{p}_{P*}(\mathfrak{q}_{P}^{*}(A)\otimes\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}}).

Similarly, we define the normalized constant term functors

\mathrm{CT}_{P*}:D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Bun}_{M},\Lambda)

A\mapsto\mathfrak{q}_{\ast}(\mathfrak{p}^{!}(-)\otimes\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}}^{-1})

and

\mathrm{CT}_{P!}:D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Bun}_{M},\Lambda)

A\mapsto\mathfrak{q}_{!}(\mathfrak{p}^{*}(A)\otimes\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}}).

If we want to emphasize the dependence on the group
G
, we will do this by using the superscript
(-)^{G}
.

Using Corollary
2.1.3
, we get evident decompositions of all of these functors. We use the superscript
(-)^{\alpha}
to denote the constituents of these decompositions, so e.g.
\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}^{\alpha}:D(\mathrm{Bun}_{M}^{\alpha},\Lambda)\to D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)
is the precomposition of
\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}
with extension by zero from the connected component labelled by
\alpha
.

For completeness, we note the following simple observation.

Lemma 2.1.8
.

There are adjunctions

\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}\dashv\mathrm{CT}_{P*}

and

\mathrm{CT}_{P!}\dashv\mathrm{Eis}_{P*}

Proof.

The first adjunction follows immediately from the definitions, and the second adjunction follows from the definitions and the identifications:
\mathfrak{q}_{P}^{!}(-)\simeq\mathfrak{q}_{P}^{*}(-)\otimes\mathfrak{q}_{P}^{*}(\Delta_{P})=\mathfrak{q}_{P}^{*}(-)\otimes\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}}^{\otimes 2}
guaranteed by Corollary
2.1.6
.
∎

We now give some more flavor for how these functors behave.

2.2.
M
-reducible elements and stalks of Eisenstein series

In this section, we will describe the stalks of the geometric Eisenstein and constant term functors in a variety of situations that will be relevant for our arguments later. We will begin with the easiest case.

Corollary 2.2.1
.

We have identifications

(1)

\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}i^{M}_{1!}(-)\simeq i_{1!}i_{P}^{G}(-)

(2)

\mathrm{Eis}_{P*}i^{M}_{1*}(-)\simeq i_{1*}i_{P}^{G}(-)

(3)

i_{1}^{*,M}\mathrm{CT}_{P!}(-)\simeq r_{P}^{G}i_{1}^{*}(-)

(4)

i_{1}^{*,M}\mathrm{CT}_{P*}(-)\simeq r_{P^{-}}^{G}i_{1}^{*}(-)

In the last part,
P^{-}\subset G
denotes any parabolic opposite to
P
.

Proof.

We have an identification
\mathrm{Bun}_{P}^{1_{M}}\simeq[\ast/\underline{P(E)}]
, which follows from HN-slope considerations. The first two isomorphisms follow easily from this identification combined with smooth and proper base-change, noting that the map
[\ast/\underline{P(E)}]\rightarrow[\ast/\underline{G(E)}]
is proper. The last two isomorphism follow by passing to left/right adjoints via Lemma
2.1.8
, Frobenius reciprocity, and classical second adjointness
[
DHKM24
, Corollary 1.3]
.
∎

Remark 2.2.2
.

In particular, this result (and its proof) showcases the direct connection between geometric second adjointness (Theorem
1.2.1
) and classical second adjointness.

We will now move into a slightly more complicated situation. In order to formulate our results properly, we need to properly take into account certain modulus character twists. To do this, we will introduce the following renormalized functors. We recall
i_{b\sharp}
, the left adjoint of the
*
-pullback considered in
[
FS21
, Proposition VII.7.2]
.

Definition 2.2.3
.

For
b\in B(G)
, we let
\delta_{b}:G_{b}(E)\rightarrow\Lambda^{*}
be the character defined in
[
HI23
, Definition 3.14]
, which by our choice of
\sqrt{q}
has a distinguished square root. For
?\in\{\sharp,*,!\}
, we define

i_{b?}^{\mathrm{ren}}(-):=i_{b?}^{\mathrm{ren}}(-\otimes\delta_{b}^{-1/2})[-\langle 2\rho_{G},\nu_{b}\rangle]

Similarly, for
?\in\{*,!\}
, we define

i_{b}^{\mathrm{ren}?}(-):=i_{b}^{?}(-\otimes\delta_{b}^{1/2})[\langle 2\rho_{G},\nu_{b}\rangle].

We note that we have natural adjunctions

i_{b!}^{\mathrm{ren}}\vdash i_{b}^{\mathrm{ren}!}

and

i_{b\sharp}^{\mathrm{ren}}\vdash i_{b}^{\mathrm{ren}*}\vdash i_{b*}^{\mathrm{ren}}

inherited from the usual adjunction relationships.

The point is that while one has a canonical identification
D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G}^{b},\Lambda)=D(G_{b}(E),\Lambda)
as in
[
FS21
, Proposition VII.7.1]
, the space
\tilde{G}_{b}
carries an
\ell
-adically contractible unipotent part whose compactly supported cohomology/dualizing complex carries a non-trivial
G_{b}(E)
-structure. In particular, these twists give rise to the relationship

\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{Verd}}i_{b!}^{\mathrm{ren}}\simeq i_{b*}^{\mathrm{ren}}\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{sm}}

using
[
HI23
, Corollary 1.6]
, where
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{sm}}
denotes smooth duality.

Assume now that
G
is quasisplit and fix a Borel
B\subset G
as well as a maximal torus
T\subset B
. In that case, for any
b\in B(G)
, the slope homomorphism
\nu_{b}
has a unique representative as a dominant rational cocharacter of
T
. For a fixed
b\in B(G)
, we let
G_{\{b\}}
denote the centralizer of
\nu_{b}
. We recall, by
[
Kot85
, Propositions 6.2,6.3]
that there exists a unique basic reduction of
b\in B(G)
to
B(G_{\{b\}})
, which we abusively also denote by
b
, satisfying the property that
\nu_{b,G_{\{b\}}}
is dominant with respect to the choice of Borel; in other words,
\nu_{b,G_{\{b\}}}
agrees with
\nu_{b}
, as rational cocharacters of
T
. The basic reduction has the additional feature that
(G_{\{b\}})_{b}\simeq G_{b}
. In particular,
G_{\{b\}}
is a quasi-split inner form of
G_{b}
. We take
P_{\{b\}}
to be the dynamic parabolic of the slope cocharacter
\nu_{b}
, which is the standard parabolic with Levi
G_{\{b\}}
. Let
P^{-}_{\{b\}}
be the opposite parabolic.

If we write
\mathrm{Bun}_{P_{\{b\}}}^{b}
and
\mathrm{Bun}_{P^{-}_{\{b\}}}^{b}
for the fibers of
\mathfrak{q}_{P_{\{b\}}}
and
\mathfrak{q}_{P_{\{b\}}^{-}}
over
i_{b}^{G_{\{b\}}}:\mathrm{Bun}_{G_{\{b\}}}^{b}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Bun}_{G_{\{b\}}}
, respectively, then we have isomorphisms

\mathrm{Bun}_{P_{\{b\}}}^{b}\simeq\mathcal{M}_{b}

and

\mathrm{Bun}_{P_{\{b\}}^{-}}^{b}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{Bun}_{G}^{b},

as in
[
HI23
, Corollary 1.6]
. Here
\pi_{b}:\mathcal{M}_{b}\rightarrow\mathrm{Bun}_{G}
is the cohomologically smooth chart of
\mathrm{Bun}_{G}
considered in
[
FS21
, Section V.3]
. Moreover, the map
\mathfrak{q}
induces the natural maps
q_{b}:\mathcal{M}_{b}\rightarrow\mathrm{Bun}_{G_{\{b\}}}^{b}\simeq[\ast/\underline{G_{b}(E)}]
and
p_{b}:\mathrm{Bun}_{G}^{b}\simeq[\ast/\tilde{G}_{b}]\rightarrow[\ast/\underline{G_{b}(E)}]
considered in
[
FS21
]
.

Remark 2.2.4
.

We note that, since
\nu_{b}
is dominant and
P_{\{b\}}
is a standard parabolic, we would expect that
\mathrm{Bun}_{P_{\{b\}}}^{b}
should parametrize split
P_{\{b\}}
-structures and not
\mathrm{Bun}_{P_{\{b\}}^{-}}^{b}
. This counter intuitive behavior comes from the minus sign when passing between
G
-bundle and isocrystals, which leads to the Harder-Narasimhan slopes of the bundle
\mathcal{F}_{b}
being the negatives of the isocrystal slopes attached to
b
. One could of course normalize this sign away, but then one would run into discrepancies when comparing with the literature on local and global Shimura varieties.

We obtain the following description of the functors described above.

Corollary 2.2.5
.

With notation as above, we have the following isomorphisms. Note that for
i_{b}^{G_{\{b\}}}
, the renormalized functors agree with the standard functors as
b\in B(G_{\{b\}})_{\mathrm{basic}}
.

(1)

\mathrm{Eis}_{P_{\{b\}}^{-}!}i_{b!}^{G_{\{b\}}}(-)\simeq i_{b!}^{\mathrm{ren}}(-)

(2)

\mathrm{Eis}_{P_{\{b\}}^{-}*}i_{b\ast}^{G_{\{b\}}}(-)\simeq i_{b*}^{\mathrm{ren}}(-)

(3)

i_{b}^{G_{\{b\}}*}\mathrm{CT}_{P_{\{b\}}^{-}*}(-)\simeq i_{b}^{\mathrm{ren}!}(-)

(4)

i_{b}^{G_{\{b\}}*}\mathrm{CT}_{P_{\{b\}}^{-}!}(-)\simeq i_{b}^{\mathrm{ren}*}(-)

(5)

\mathrm{Eis}_{P_{\{b\}}!}i_{b!}^{G_{\{b\}}}(-)\simeq i_{b\sharp}^{\mathrm{ren}}(-)
.

Proof.

The first and second isomorphisms follow easily from the isomorphism
\mathrm{Bun}_{P_{\{b\}}^{-}}^{b}\simeq[\ast/\tilde{G}_{b}]
, and the cohomological smoothness of
\mathfrak{q}_{P}
. Recall that the identification
D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G}^{b},\Lambda)\simeq D(G_{b}(E),\Lambda)
is induced by the pullback map
p_{b}:\mathrm{Bun}_{G}^{b}\simeq[\ast/\tilde{G}_{b}]\rightarrow[\ast/\underline{G_{b}(E)}]
.

The next two isomorphisms follow from the first by passing to adjoints via Lemma
2.1.8
. It remains to justify the fifth isomorphism. To do this we note, by
[
FS21
, Proposition VII.7.2]
, that the left adjoint
i_{b\sharp}
is isomorphic to
\pi_{b\natural}q_{b}^{*}
, where
\pi_{b\natural}
is the left adjoint of
\pi_{b}^{*}
. However, by
[
FS21
, Theorem V.3.7]
, the map
\pi_{b}
is cohomologically smooth and therefore it follows that
\pi_{b!}(-\otimes\pi_{b}^{!}(\Lambda))\simeq\pi_{b\natural}(-)
. However, we note by
[
HI23
, Proposition 1.1]
, the dualizing complex on
\mathrm{Bun}_{G}
is the constant sheaf, and therefore it follows that
\pi_{b}^{!}(\Lambda)
identifies with the dualizing complex of
\mathcal{M}_{b}
, which by
[
HI23
, Corollary 1.6]
is isomorphic to
q_{b}^{*}(\delta_{b})[2\langle 2\rho_{G},\nu_{b}\rangle]
. Therefore, we deduce that
\pi_{b!}q_{b}^{*}(-\otimes\delta_{b})[2\langle 2\rho_{G},\nu_{b}\rangle]\simeq i_{b\sharp}(-)
, which implies that

\pi_{b!}q_{b}^{*}(-\otimes\delta_{b}^{1/2})[\langle 2\rho_{G},\nu_{b}\rangle]\simeq i_{b\sharp}^{\mathrm{ren}}(-),

but the LHS is precisely
\mathrm{Eis}_{P_{b}!}i_{b!}^{G_{\{b\}}}
, by the above discussion and proper base-change.
∎

Remark 2.2.6
.

We note that in this particular case Theorem
1.2.1
(3) can be deduced from the adjunctions

i_{b!}^{\mathrm{ren}}\vdash i_{b}^{\mathrm{ren}!}

and

i_{b\sharp}^{\mathrm{ren}}\vdash i_{b}^{\mathrm{ren}*}\vdash i_{b*}^{\mathrm{ren}}.

In the remainder of this section, we combine the previous propositions to give a description of the stalks of geometric Eisenstein series in certain simple cases. More precisely, we evaluate
\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}
when restricted to basic strata of
\mathrm{Bun}_{M}
that are either dominant or anti-dominant with respect to
P
. We continue to assume that
G
is quasisplit, and fix
T\subset B\subset G
. We first recall a combinatorial description of the following set, for some standard Levi
M
of
G
.

Definition 2.2.7
.

We define
B(G)_{M}:=\mathrm{Im}(i_{M}:B(M)_{\mathrm{basic}}\rightarrow B(G))
, the set of elements of
B(G)
admitting a basic reduction to
M
.

For
b\in B(G)_{M}
, we consider the collection of Weyl group elements
W_{M,b}:=W[M,G_{\{b\}}]
, as defined in
[
BMO22
, Section 5.3]
. We recall the definition: Let
W_{G}
denote the (relative) Weyl group of
G
, i.e. the quotient

W_{G}=N(T)(E)/T(E).

Then
W[M,G_{\{b\}}]\subset W_{G}
is the set of elements
w\in W_{G}
such that

w(M)\subset G_{\{b\}}

w^{-1}(G_{\{b\}}\cap B)\subset B.

We will use the following notions of dominance throughout the paper. Let
\mathbb{X}_{*}(M^{\mathrm{ab}}_{\overline{E}})_{\Gamma}^{+}\subset\mathbb{X}_{*}(M^{\mathrm{ab}}_{\overline{E}})_{\Gamma}
denote the positive Weyl chamber inside the coinvariant lattice for a standard Levi
M
, where this is defined with respect to our fixed choice of Borel.

Definition 2.2.8
.

We say an element
\theta\in B(M)_{\mathrm{basic}}
is
dominant
if
\overline{\theta}
lies in the positive Weyl chamber
\mathbb{X}_{*}(M^{\mathrm{ab}}_{\overline{E}})_{\Gamma}^{+}\subset\mathbb{X}_{*}(M^{\mathrm{ab}}_{\overline{E}})_{\Gamma}
. Similarly, if it lies in the negative Weyl chamber we say that it is
anti-dominant
.

Remark 2.2.9
.

The previous definition applies more generally for any reductive group
G
equipped with a parabolic
P\subset G
with Levi
M
: We say that an element
\theta\in B(M)_{\mathrm{basic}}
is
P
-dominant
(resp.
P
-antidominant
) if
\kappa_{M}(\theta)
lies in the positive (resp. negative) Weyl chamber defined with respect to any choice of Borel
B\subset P_{\overline{E}}
.
5
5

5

Note that as
\theta
is assumed to be basic, this notion depends only on
P
and not the choice of Borel
B\subset P_{\overline{E}}
.

We have the following lemma.

Lemma 2.2.10
.

[
HI23
, Lemma 4.23]

For every element
b\in B(G)_{M}
, there exists an injective map of sets

\displaystyle i_{M}^{-1}(b)

\displaystyle\rightarrow W_{M,b}

\displaystyle b_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\mapsto w_{\alpha}

that sends
b_{\alpha}
attached to
\alpha\in\pi_{1}(M)_{\Gamma}
to the unique
w_{\alpha}\in W_{M,b}
such that
w_{\alpha}(b_{\alpha})\in B(w_{\alpha}(M))_{\mathrm{basic}}
is dominant. It follows that
w_{\alpha}(b_{\alpha})\in B(w_{\alpha}(M))_{\mathrm{basic}}
is a reduction of
b\in B(G_{\{b\}})_{\mathrm{basic}}
to
B(w_{\alpha}(M))_{\mathrm{basic}}
. Moreover, the image of this map is given by the set of
w\in W_{M,b}
such that
w(M)\subset G_{\{b\}}
transfers to a Levi subgroup of
G_{b}
under the inner twisting
(G_{\{b\}})_{b}\simeq G_{b}
.

The element
w_{\alpha}
will be important for describing the stalks of the Eisenstein functor. We will also need the following complementary lemma. Here
P
denotes the standard parabolic with Levi
M
.

Lemma 2.2.11
.

[
HI23
, Lemma 4.24]

An element
b\in B(G)
lies in
B(G)_{M}
if and only if there exists
w\in W_{M,b}
such that the parabolic
w(P)\cap G_{\{b\}}
of
G_{\{b\}}
transfers to a parabolic subgroup
Q_{b,w}\subset G_{b}
under the inner twisting between
G_{\{b\}}
and
G_{b}
. More precisely, if
b_{\alpha}
maps to
b\in B(G)
with corresponding Weyl group element
w_{\alpha}
as in the previous lemma then
w_{\alpha}(P)\cap G_{\{b\}}
transfers to a parabolic subgroup of
G_{\{b\}}
. Moreover, for every element
\alpha\in B(M)_{\mathrm{basic}}
mapping to
b
, the Levi factor of
Q_{b,w_{\alpha}}
is equal to
w_{\alpha}(M)_{w_{\alpha}(b_{\alpha})}
.

We write

(-)^{w_{\alpha}}:D(M_{b_{\alpha}},\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\simeq}D(w_{\alpha}(M)_{w_{\alpha}(b_{\alpha})},\Lambda)

for the equivalence induced by the isomorphism
M_{b_{\alpha}}\simeq w_{\alpha}(M)_{w_{\alpha}(b_{\alpha})}
given by
w_{\alpha}
. We write
(-)^{w_{\alpha}^{-1}}
for its inverse.

We now have the following, which is simply a reinterpretation of
[
HI23
, Theorem 4.26]
in terms of the renormalized pushforwards.

Proposition 2.2.12
.

For all
\alpha\in\pi_{1}(M)_{\Gamma}
corresponding to
b_{\alpha}\in B(M)_{\mathrm{basic}}
mapping to
b\in B(G)_{M}
with associated Weyl group element
w_{\alpha}\in W_{M,b}
, we consider
A\in D(M_{b_{\alpha}}(E),\Lambda)
. Then we have an isomorphism

i_{b}^{\mathrm{ren}*}\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}i_{b_{\alpha}!}(A)\simeq i_{Q_{b,w_{\alpha}}}^{G_{b}}(A^{w_{\alpha}})

in
D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)
. Moreover, if
b_{\alpha}
is anti-dominant then this defines an isomorphism

\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}i_{b_{\alpha!}}(-)\simeq i_{b!}^{\mathrm{ren}}i_{Q_{b,w_{\alpha}}}^{G_{b}}(-)^{w_{\alpha}}:D(M_{b_{\alpha}}(E),\Lambda)\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)

where
w_{\alpha}
is a minimal length representative of the element of longest length.

Using this, we can also deduce the following description of the functor
\mathrm{CT}_{P*}
.

Corollary 2.2.13
.

With notation as in Proposition
2.2.12
, we consider
\alpha\in\pi_{1}(M)_{\Gamma}
such that
b_{\alpha}
is anti-dominant. Then we have the following identification of functors

i_{b_{\alpha}}^{*}\mathrm{CT}_{P*}(-)\simeq(r_{Q_{b,w_{\alpha}}^{-}}^{G}i_{b}^{\mathrm{ren}!}(-))^{w_{\alpha}^{-1}}:D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)\rightarrow D(M_{b_{\alpha}}(E),\Lambda).

Proof.

This follows from the previous proposition by passing to right adjoints and using Lemma
2.1.8
as well as usual second adjointness
[
DHKM24
, Corollary 1.3]
.
∎

We can also give a more general description of the dominant case.

Corollary 2.2.14
.

Let
\alpha\in\pi_{1}(M)_{\Gamma}
be a basic element such that
b_{\alpha}\in B(M)_{\mathrm{basic}}
is dominant and maps to
b\in B(G)
. Then
w_{\alpha}=1
and we have natural isomorphisms

\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}i_{b_{\alpha}!}\simeq i_{b\sharp}^{\mathrm{ren}}\circ i_{Q_{b,1}}^{G_{b}}:D(M_{b_{\alpha}}(E),\Lambda)\rightarrow D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)

and

i_{b_{\alpha}}^{\ast}\mathrm{CT}_{P\ast}\simeq r_{Q_{b,1}^{-}}^{G_{b}}i_{b}^{\mathrm{ren}\ast}:D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)\rightarrow D(M_{b_{\alpha}}(E),\Lambda).

Proof.

Using compatibility of Eisenstein functors with composition, along
P\subset P_{\{b\}}\subset G
, the first statement is a combination of Corollary
2.2.1
(1) and Corollary
2.2.5
(5). The second statement follows by passing to right adjoints.
∎

3.
Finiteness conditions and first results

In this section, we will begin our study of the finiteness properties of the functors
\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}
and
\mathrm{Eis}_{P*}
. Here,
G
denotes a reductive group over
E
with parabolic subgroup
P\subset G
and Levi quotient
M
. We do not assume that
G
is quasisplit.

3.1.
Preservation of compact objects

We recall that the derived category
D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)
has two natural full subcategories of sheaves satisfying certain finiteness properties.

Definition 3.1.1
.

(1)

There is the full subcategory
D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)^{\omega}
of compact objects. Equivalently, by
[
FS21
, Theorem I.5.1 (iii)]
, these are the objects
A\in D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)
which have quasi-compact support, and their restriction to the HN-strata
i_{b}^{*}(A)\in D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G}^{b},\Lambda)\simeq D(G_{b}(E),\Lambda)
defines a compact object, where this is equivalent to lying in the thick triangulated subcategory generated by objects
\mathrm{cInd}_{K}^{G_{b}(E)}(\Lambda)
for
K\subset G_{b}(E)
an open pro-
p
subgroup.

(2)

There is the full subcategory of
D^{\mathrm{ULA}}(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)
of objects which are ULA over
\ast
. Equivalently, by
[
FS21
, Theorem I.5.1 (v)]
, these are the objects
A\in D(G_{b}(E),\Lambda)
whose restriction
i_{b}^{*}(A)
to the HN-strata
D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G}^{b},\Lambda)\simeq D(G_{b}(E),\Lambda)
are admissible. In other words,
i_{b}^{*}(A)^{K}
is a perfect complex for all open pro-
p
subgroups
K\subset G_{b}(E)
.

We will now commence our study on how these subcategories interact with the Eisenstein and Constant Term functors. In this section, we prove the following theorem.

Theorem 3.1.2
.

The functor
\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}
preserves compact objects.

Corollary 3.1.3
.

The functor
\mathrm{CT}_{P\ast}
commutes with all limits and colimits, and preserves ULA objects.

Proof.

Since
\mathrm{CT}_{P\ast}
is a right adjoint, it clearly commutes with limits. Since its left adjoint preserves compact objects by Theorem
3.1.2
, it also commutes with colimits. For preservation of ULA objects, we observe more generally that if
L:D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)\leftrightarrows D(\mathrm{Bun}_{H},\Lambda):R
are a pair of adjoint functors for some reductive groups
G,H
such that
L
preserves compact objects, then
R
preserves ULA objects. This follows immediately from the characterization of ULA objects implicit in
[
FS21
, Prop. VII.7.4 and Prop. VII.7.9]
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.1.2
.

Assume first that
G
is quasisplit, and fix
T\subset B\subset G
as usual.
By induction, we can assume that the theorem is true for all proper Levi subgroups of
G
. We will prove the following claim by induction on quasicompact open substacks
U\subset\mathrm{Bun}_{G}
(equivalently, finite subsets
|U|\subset|\mathrm{Bun}_{G}|=B(G)
stable under generization).

Claim 3.1.4
.

For any standard Levi
M\subset G
and any
b\in B(M)
mapping into
|U|\subset B(G)
, and any (semistandard) parabolic
P
with Levi
M
, the functor

\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}\circ i_{b!}^{\mathrm{ren},M}:D(M(E),\Lambda)\to D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)

preserves compact objects.

Consider a general pair
(P,b)
as above. Our goal is to show that
\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}\circ i_{b!}^{M}
preserves compact objects.
By induction, we can assume that
\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{\prime}!}\circ i_{b^{\prime}!}^{M^{\prime}}
preserves compact objects for every pair
(P^{\prime},b^{\prime})
such that the image of
b^{\prime}
in
B(G)
is
strictly
more semistable than the image of
b
.

First, by reducing
b
further to a basic element of a Levi subgroup of
M
, and using compatibility of Eisenstein functors with composition and Corollary
2.2.5
(3), we can reduce to the case that
b=b_{\alpha}\in B(M)
is basic. Then there is the Levi subgroup
G_{\{b\}}\subset G
and the element
w_{\alpha}\in W[M,G_{\{b\}}]
. We have
M\subset w_{\alpha}^{-1}(G_{\{b\}})
, and the image of
b
in
B(w_{\alpha}^{-1}(G_{\{b\}}))
stays basic. We can then use compatibility of Eisenstein functors with composition again to reduce to the case
M=w_{\alpha}^{-1}(G_{\{b\}})
. As
P
was an arbitrary parabolic with Levi
M
, we can undo the Weyl group twist. In other words, we can assume that
M=G_{\{b\}}
. We have to show that for all parabolics
P
with Levi
M=G_{\{b\}}
, the functor

\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}\circ i_{b!}^{M}

preserves compact objects. We note that this is true for the standard parabolic, by Corollary
2.2.14
and the preservation of compact objects under parabolic induction and the functors
i_{b\sharp}
.

We will now show that the result is true for some parabolic
P
with Levi
M
if and only if it is true for any other
P^{\prime}
. Moving from one parabolic with Levi
M
to any other via a sequence of minimal modifications, we can assume that
P
and
P^{\prime}
are contained in some parabolic
Q
whose Levi
N\supset M
has the property that
P\cap N
and
P^{\prime}\cap N
are maximal parabolic subgroups. In particular, one of them is standard and the other is anti-standard in
N
; without loss of generality
P\cap N
is the standard one. Then

\mathrm{Eis}_{P\cap N!}\circ i_{b!}^{M}=i_{b\sharp}^{\mathrm{ren},N}

and

\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{\prime}\cap N!}\circ i_{b!}^{M}=i_{b!}^{\mathrm{ren},N}

by Corollary
2.2.5
(5) resp. (1). In particular, there is a triangle

i_{b\sharp}^{\mathrm{ren},N}\to i_{b!}^{\mathrm{ren},N}\to C

where the cone
C
is supported at points of
\mathrm{Bun}_{N}
whose image in
\mathrm{Bun}_{G}
is strictly more semistable than
b
, using the Lemma below. Moreover, the functor
C
preserves compact objects.

Applying
\mathrm{Eis}_{Q!}
to the last displayed triangle, and using the isomorphisms before as well as the composability of Eisenstein functors, we get a triangle

\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}\circ i_{b!}^{M}\to\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{\prime}!}\circ i_{b!}^{M}\to\mathrm{Eis}_{Q!}\circ C.

By induction,
\mathrm{Eis}_{Q!}\circ C
preserves compact objects. It follows that
\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}\circ i_{b!}^{M}
preserves compact objects if and only if
\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{\prime}!}\circ i_{b!}^{M}
does, as desired.

This finishes the case that
G
is quasisplit. If
G
has connected center, then so does
M
, and then there is a basic element of
M
such that
M_{b}
is quasisplit, hence so is
G_{b}
. Twisting everything by
b
and using
\mathrm{Bun}_{M_{b}}\cong\mathrm{Bun}_{M}
etc., this reduces the case of
G
to the case of
G_{b}
.

It remains to reduce the case of a general
G
to the case of a
G
with connected center. One can always find a z-extension

1\to G\to\tilde{G}\to D\to 1

where
D
is a torus and
\tilde{G}
has connected center (by embedding the center
Z_{G}
of
G
into a torus, and taking a pushout). In that case,

\mathrm{Bun}_{G}=\mathrm{Bun}_{\tilde{G}}\times_{\mathrm{Bun}_{D}}\ast

and the corresponding functor

D(\mathrm{Bun}_{\tilde{G}},\Lambda)\otimes_{D(\mathrm{Bun}_{D},\Lambda)}D(\Lambda)\to D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)

is an equivalence. Any parabolic
P
lifts uniquely to a parabolic
\tilde{P}
of
\tilde{G}
, the functor
\mathrm{Eis}_{\tilde{P}!}
is
D(\mathrm{Bun}_{D},\Lambda)
-linear and as it preserves compact objects by the case already established, its right adjoint is automatically colimit-preserving and also
D(\mathrm{Bun}_{D},\Lambda)
-linear. (The
D(\mathrm{Bun}_{D},\Lambda)
-linearity can either be verified by hand, or deduced from the second adjunction proved in the next section.) Then the same follows for
\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}=\mathrm{Eis}_{\tilde{P}!}\otimes_{D(\mathrm{Bun}_{D},\Lambda)}D(\Lambda)
, as desired.
∎

In the preceding argument we used the following lemma, which is probably
well-known.

Lemma 3.1.5
.

If
M\subset G
is a standard Levi and
b,b^{\prime}\in B(M)
are any elements
such that
b\prec^{M}b^{\prime}
, then their images in
B(G)
satisfy
b\prec^{G}b^{\prime}
.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that the natural map on slope polygons is induced by the natural quotient map
\mathbb{X}_{*}(T_{\overline{E}})/W_{M_{\overline{E}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{X}_{*}(T_{\overline{E}})/W_{G_{\overline{E}}}
, where
W_{G_{\overline{E}}}
(resp.
W_{M_{\overline{E}}}
) denotes the absolute Weyl group of
G
(resp.
M
). In particular, if two elements in
\mathbb{X}_{*}(T_{\overline{E}})/W_{M_{\overline{E}}}
differ by a non-zero (resp. zero) linear combination of positive absolute roots of
M
then the same is true for their image in
\mathbb{X}_{*}(T_{\overline{E}})/W_{G_{\overline{E}}}
. The claim follows.
∎

3.2.
Second adjointness

In this section, we prove the following theorem. We stress that we are not assuming that
G
is quasisplit (or fixing a choice of Borel for that matter).

Theorem 3.2.1
.

Let
P,P^{-}\subset G
be a pair of opposite parabolics with Levi
M=P\cap P^{-}
.

(1)

There is a natural isomorphism of functors

\mathrm{CT}_{P^{-}\ast}\cong\mathrm{CT}_{P!}:D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)\to D(\mathrm{Bun}_{M},\Lambda).

(2)

There is a natural isomorphism of functors

\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}\cong\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{-}!}\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}^{M}:D(\mathrm{Bun}_{M},\Lambda)^{\omega}\to D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)^{\omega}.

Note the restriction in part (2) to compact sheaves, in which case the statement is well-posed by the main result in the previous section.

Both parts of this theorem can be interpreted as geometrizations of Bernstein’s famous second adjointness theorem, and, as mentioned in Remark
2.2.2
, our result specializes to classical second adjointness. However, this is completely circular, as we will use classical second adjointness in the proof, via our dependence on
[
DHKM24
]
.

In the argument below, we will first show that (1) and (2) are logically equivalent, in a precise sense. In an ideal world, we would then directly prove (1) as an application of hyperbolic localization, as in
[
DG16
]
. However, we were unable to make such an argument work.
6
6

6

Perhaps this is inevitable. In some sense, there cannot be an argument for (1) which is completely formal, e.g. making use only of the fact that our sheaf category underlies a six-functor formalism with excision, since (1) fails with mod-
p
coefficients.
Instead, we will prove (2) by an excision argument parallel to the proof that
\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}
preserves compact objects given in the previous section.

We begin by constructing a canonical natural transformation

\mathrm{CT}_{P^{-}\ast}\to\mathrm{CT}_{P!}.

It is enough to construct a natural transformation

\mathrm{id}\to\mathrm{CT}_{P!}\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{-}!}

as then the desired transformation arises via precomposition with the functor
\mathrm{CT}_{P^{-}\ast}
and postcomposition with the counit map
\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{-}!}\mathrm{CT}_{P^{-}\ast}\to\mathrm{id}
. Now
\mathrm{CT}_{P!}\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{-}!}
is given by a kernel, namely the fiber product

\mathrm{Bun}_{P^{-}}\times_{\mathrm{Bun}_{G}}\mathrm{Bun}_{P}\to\mathrm{Bun}_{M}\times\mathrm{Bun}_{M},

with the sheaf
\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P^{-}}}\boxtimes\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}}
. This naturally contains the diagonal
\mathrm{Bun}_{M}
as an open substack, yielding the desired map
\mathrm{id}\to\mathrm{CT}_{P!}\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{-}!}
once observing that the sheaf
\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P^{-}}}\boxtimes\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}}
restricts to the trivial sheaf on
\mathrm{Bun}_{M}
.

Let us note some equivalent descriptions of the same map. First, one can also construct a natural transformation
t:\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}\to\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{-}!}\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}^{M}
. For this we will make reference to the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{Bun}_{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}\times_{\mathrm{Bun}_{G}}\mathrm{Bun}_{P^{-}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\;\;\;\;f}
\scriptstyle{f^{-}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Bun}_{P^{-}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{p}^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{q}^{-}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Bun}_{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{M}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{q}}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{p}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Bun}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{G}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Bun}_{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{M}}
\textstyle{\ast}

of Artin v-stacks that was already implicitly used above. Note that the inner square is Cartesian,
j
is an open immersion, and the unlabelled slanted arrows are the identity on
\mathrm{Bun}_{M}
.

For compact sheaves
A,B
on
\mathrm{Bun}_{M}
, we have a canonical isomorphism

R\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}A,\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{-}!}B)\cong\pi_{G!}(\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}A\otimes\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{-}!}B)

by the isomorphism characterizing Bernstein-Zelevinsky duality on
\mathrm{Bun}_{G}
(
[
FS21
, Proposition VII.7.6]
, where we are implicitly using
[
HI23
, Proposition 1.1]
to identify
\pi_{\natural}
and
\pi_{!}
). We will now construct a canonical map

\gamma:\pi_{M!}(A\otimes B)\to\pi_{G!}(\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}A\otimes\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{-}!}B)

functorial in
A
and
B
. Using again the isomorphism characterizing Bernstein-Zelevinsky duality on
\mathrm{Bun}_{M}
, the source identifies canonically with
R\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}^{M}A,B)
,
so composing we get a functorial map

R\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}^{M}A,B)\to R\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}A,\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{-}!}B).

When
B=\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}^{M}A
, the source contains the obvious identity map, and its image in (
H^{0}
of) the target induces the desired natural transformation
t
.

To construct
\gamma
, the key observation is that, using the Künneth formula, there is a canonical isomorphism

\pi_{G!}(\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}A\otimes\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{-}!}B)\cong\pi_{X!}(f^{-\ast}\mathfrak{q}^{\ast}A\otimes f^{-\ast}\mathfrak{q}^{-\ast}B\otimes C),

where we set
X=\mathrm{Bun}_{P}\times_{\mathrm{Bun}_{G}}\mathrm{Bun}_{P^{-}}
and
C=f^{-\ast}\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}}\otimes f^{\ast}\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P^{-}}}
for brevity. Since
j
is an open immersion, the adjunction
j_{!}j^{\ast}\to\mathrm{id}
induces a canonical map

\pi_{X!}j_{!}j^{*}(f^{-\ast}\mathfrak{q}^{\ast}A\otimes f^{\ast}\mathfrak{q}^{-\ast}B\otimes C)\to\pi_{X!}(f^{-\ast}\mathfrak{q}^{\ast}A\otimes f^{\ast}\mathfrak{q}^{-\ast}B\otimes C).

Using that
\pi_{X!}j_{!}=\pi_{M!}
,
j^{*}(f^{-\ast}\mathfrak{q}^{\ast}A\otimes f^{\ast}\mathfrak{q}^{-\ast}B)=A\otimes B
, and
j^{\ast}C=\Lambda
, the source identifies with
\pi_{M!}(A\otimes B)
. This gives the desired map
\gamma
.

Next we explain how the transformation
t
canonically induces a natural transformation
s:\mathrm{CT}_{P^{-}\ast}\to\mathrm{CT}_{P!}
. Given
A\in D(\mathrm{Bun}_{M},\Lambda)^{\omega}
and
B\in D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)
, we have a natural isomorphism

R\mathrm{Hom}(\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{-}!}\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}^{M}A,B)\cong R\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}^{M}A,\mathrm{CT}_{P^{-}\ast}B)

by the evident adjunction between
\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{-}!}
and
\mathrm{CT}_{P^{-}\ast}
. On the other hand, we have canonical isomorphisms

\displaystyle R\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}A,B)

\displaystyle\cong\pi_{!}(\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}A\otimes B)

\displaystyle\cong\pi_{!}(\mathfrak{p}_{!}(\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}}\otimes\mathfrak{q}^{\ast}A)\otimes B)

\displaystyle\cong(\pi\circ\mathfrak{p})_{!}(\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}}\otimes\mathfrak{q}^{\ast}A\otimes\mathfrak{p}^{\ast}B)

\displaystyle\cong(\pi_{M}\circ\mathfrak{q})_{!}(\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}}\otimes\mathfrak{q}^{\ast}A\otimes\mathfrak{p}^{\ast}B)

\displaystyle\cong\pi_{M!}(A\otimes\mathfrak{q}_{!}(\mathrm{IC}_{\mathrm{Bun}_{P}}\otimes\mathfrak{p}^{\ast}B))

\displaystyle\cong\pi_{M!}(A\otimes\mathrm{CT}_{P!}B)

\displaystyle\cong R\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}^{M}A,\mathrm{CT}_{P!}B).

Here the first and last lines follow from the isomorphisms characterizing Bernstein-Zelevinsky duality, and the remaining lines follow from unwinding definitions and liberally applying the projection formula. Combining these calculations, we see that the transformation
t
induces a natural map

\displaystyle R\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}^{M}A,\mathrm{CT}_{P^{-}\ast}B)

\displaystyle\cong R\mathrm{Hom}(\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{-}!}\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}^{M}A,B)

\displaystyle\to R\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}A,B)

\displaystyle\cong R\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}^{M}A,\mathrm{CT}_{P!}B).

By Yoneda, this induces the desired transformation
s
. One checks that this is equivalent to the map
\mathrm{CT}_{P^{-}\ast}\to\mathrm{CT}_{P!}
constructed at the beginning. It is also clear that if
t
is an equivalence, then
s
is an equivalence, and conversely. In particular, to prove Theorem
3.2.1
, it is enough to show that
t
is an equivalence.

We also need some basic compatibility properties of the transformation
t
. Since we will begin varying the parabolic, we write
t_{P}
to indicate the dependence on the parabolic
P
when necessary, and similarly for the map
\gamma
.

Proposition 3.2.2
.

The transformation
t
is compatible with composition of parabolics. More precisely, suppose given a parabolic
P_{1}
with Levi quotient
M_{1}
, and let
P_{2}\subset M_{1}
be a parabolic with Levi quotient
M_{2}
. Let
Q=P_{1}\times_{M_{1}}P_{2}
be the evident parabolic in
G
with Levi quotient
M_{2}
. Then the composition

\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}\mathrm{Eis}_{Q!}\cong\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}\mathrm{Eis}_{P_{1}!}\mathrm{Eis}_{P_{2}!}\to\mathrm{Eis}_{P_{1}^{-}!}\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}^{M_{1}}\mathrm{Eis}_{P_{2}!}\to\mathrm{Eis}_{P_{1}^{-}!}\mathrm{Eis}_{P_{2}^{-}!}\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}^{M_{2}}\cong\mathrm{Eis}_{Q^{-}!}\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}^{M_{2}}

agrees with
t_{Q}
, where the first arrow is induced by
t_{P_{1}}
and the second arrow is induced by
t_{P_{2}}
.

Proof.

After unwinding the definition of
t
in terms of the map
\gamma
, this reduces to the claim that for all compact sheaves
A,B
on
\mathrm{Bun}_{M_{2}}
, the composition

\displaystyle\pi_{M_{2}!}(A\otimes B)\overset{\gamma_{P_{2}}}{\to}\pi_{M_{1}!}(\mathrm{Eis}_{P_{2}!}A\otimes\mathrm{Eis}_{P_{2}^{-}!}B)\overset{\gamma_{P_{1}}}{\to}\pi_{G!}(\mathrm{Eis}_{P_{1}!}\mathrm{Eis}_{P_{2}!}A\otimes\mathrm{Eis}_{P_{1}^{-}!}\mathrm{Eis}_{P_{2}^{-}!}B)

\displaystyle\cong\pi_{G!}(\mathrm{Eis}_{Q!}A\otimes\mathrm{Eis}_{Q^{-}!}B)

agrees with
\gamma_{Q}
. But these maps are all induced by kernels, more precisely the open immersions

\mathrm{Bun}_{M_{2}}\subset\mathrm{Bun}_{P_{2}^{-}}\times_{\mathrm{Bun}_{M_{1}}}\mathrm{Bun}_{P_{2}}\subset\mathrm{Bun}_{Q^{-}}\times_{\mathrm{Bun}_{G}}\mathrm{Bun}_{Q}.\qed

We also need that
t_{P}
induces an isomorphism in the following seed cases. As all maps are induced by explicit kernels, these compatibilities are easy to check by checking them on kernels.

\bullet
If
b\in B(M)
is an element whose image in
B(G)
is basic, then
\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}\circ i_{b!}^{M}\simeq i_{b!}^{G}\circ i_{P_{b}}^{G_{b}}
resp.
\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{-}!}\circ i_{b!}^{M}\simeq i_{b!}^{G}\circ i_{P_{b}^{-}}^{G_{b}}
,
and in this case precomposition of
t_{P}
with
i_{b!}^{M}
reduces to the map

\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{coh}}i_{P_{b}}^{G_{b}}\to i_{P_{b}^{-}}^{G_{b}}\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{coh}}^{M}

on compact objects which is an isomorphism as an equivalent form of the second adjointness proved in
[
DHKM24
]
.

\bullet
If
b\in B(M)
is basic and strictly dominant, then
\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}\circ i_{b!}^{M}\simeq i_{b\sharp}^{\mathrm{ren}}
and
\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{-}!}\circ i_{b!}^{M}\simeq i_{b!}^{\mathrm{ren}}
by Corollary
2.2.5
(5) and (1). In this case precomposition of
t_{P}
with
i_{b!}^{M}
reduces to the known isomorphism
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}i_{b\sharp}^{\mathrm{ren}}\cong i_{b!}^{\mathrm{ren}}\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{coh}}^{G_{b}}
. This follows from the computation of
\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}
on compact generators performed in the proof of
[
FS21
, Theorem V.5.1]
.

Proof of Theorem
3.2.1
.

Again, we assume first that
G
is quasisplit, and fix
T\subset B\subset G
. We can assume that
M
contains
T
, and so
P
and
P^{-}
are semistandard. By induction, we can assume that the theorem holds true for all proper Levi subgroups of
G
. We will prove the following claim by induction on quasicompact open substacks
U\subset\mathrm{Bun}_{G}
(equivalently, finite subsets
|U|\subset|\mathrm{Bun}_{G}|=B(G)
stable under generization).

Claim 3.2.3
.

For any standard Levi
M\subset G
, any
b\in B(M)
mapping into
|U|\subset B(G)
, and any pair of opposite parabolics
P
and
P^{-}
with Levi
M
, the natural transformation

\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}\circ\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}i_{b!}^{\mathrm{ren},M}\to\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{-}!}\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}^{M}i_{b!}^{\mathrm{ren},M}

is an equivalence on compact objects.

We follow the same steps as in the proof of Theorem
3.1.2
. By reducing
b
further to a basic element of a Levi subgroup of
M
, and using compatibility of Eisenstein functors (and the transformation
t
) with composition, we can reduce to the case that
b=b_{\alpha}\in B(M)
is basic. Then there is the Levi subgroup
G_{\{b\}}\subset G
and the element
w_{\alpha}\in W[M,G_{\{b\}}]
. We have
M\subset w_{\alpha}^{-1}(G_{\{b\}})
, and the image of
b
in
B(w_{\alpha}^{-1}(G_{\{b\}}))
stays basic. We can then use compatibility of Eisenstein functors (and the transformation
t
) with composition again to reduce to the case
M=w_{\alpha}^{-1}(G_{\{b\}})
. As
P
and
P^{-}
were arbitrary opposite parabolics with Levi
M
, we can undo the Weyl group twist. In other words, we can assume that
M=G_{\{b\}}
. We have to show that for all parabolics
P
with Levi
M=G_{\{b\}}
and opposite parabolic
P^{-}
, the transformation

\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}\circ\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}i_{b!}^{M}\to\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{-}!}\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}^{M}i_{b!}^{M}

is an equivalence on compact objects. By our seed case, we know that this is true when
P
is the standard parabolic.

We will now show that the result is true for some parabolic
P
with Levi
M
if and only if it is true for any other
P^{\prime}
. Moving from one parabolic with Levi
M
to any other via a sequence of minimal modifications, we can assume that
P
and
P^{\prime}
are contained in some parabolic
Q
whose Levi
N\supset M
has the property that
P\cap N
and
P^{\prime}\cap N
are maximal parabolic subgroups. In particular, one of them is standard and the other is anti-standard in
N
; without loss of generality
P\cap N
is the standard one. As in the proof of Theorem
3.1.2
, we have

\mathrm{Eis}_{P\cap N!}i_{b!}^{M}=i_{b\sharp}^{\mathrm{ren},N}

and

\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{\prime}\cap N!}i_{b!}^{M}=i_{b!}^{\mathrm{ren},N}

and the triangle

i_{b\sharp}^{\mathrm{ren},N}\to i_{b!}^{\mathrm{ren},N}\to C

where the cone
C
is supported at elements strictly more semistable than
b
. By induction, we know that the natural transformation

\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}\mathrm{Eis}_{Q!}\to\mathrm{Eis}_{Q^{-}!}\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}^{N}

is an equivalence on
C
. Thus, it is an equivalence on
i_{b!}^{\mathrm{ren},N}
if and only if it is an equivalence on
i_{b\sharp}^{\mathrm{ren},N}
.

Now this transformation applied to
i_{b\sharp}^{\mathrm{ren},N}=\mathrm{Eis}_{P\cap N!}i_{b!}^{M}
yields the transformation

\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}i_{b!}^{M}\to\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{-}!}\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}^{M}i_{b!}^{M}

again by compatibility with composition (and as the result is known for
N
, as
P\cap N
is standard). Similarly, the transformation applied to
i_{b!}^{\mathrm{ren},N}=\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{\prime}\cap N!}i_{b!}^{M}
yields the transformation

\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{\prime}!}i_{b!}^{M}\to\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{\prime-}!}\mathbb{D}_{\mathrm{BZ}}^{M}i_{b!}^{M}.

Thus, the transformation for
P
is an isomorphism if and only if the transformation for
P^{\prime}
is an isomorphism, as desired.

This finishes the case that
G
is quasisplit. If
G
has connected center, then so does
M
, and there is some basic element
b\in B(M)
such that
M_{b}
(and hence
G_{b}
) is quasisplit. One can then twist
P
and
P^{\prime}
by
b
to obtain opposite parabolics of
G_{b}
with Levi
M_{b}
, and this reduces the case of
G
to the case of
G_{b}
.

It remains to reduce the case of a general
G
to the case of a
G
with connected center. Again, we pick a z-extension

1\to G\to\tilde{G}\to D\to 1

where
D
is a torus and
\tilde{G}
has connected center, so

\mathrm{Bun}_{G}=\mathrm{Bun}_{\tilde{G}}\times_{\mathrm{Bun}_{D}}\ast

and the corresponding functor

D(\mathrm{Bun}_{\tilde{G}},\Lambda)\otimes_{D(\mathrm{Bun}_{D},\Lambda)}D(\Lambda)\to D(\mathrm{Bun}_{G},\Lambda)

is an equivalence. The parabolics
P
and
P^{-}
lift to parabolics
\tilde{P}
and
\tilde{P}^{-}
, and the isomorphism

\mathrm{CT}_{\tilde{P}^{-}\ast}\to\mathrm{CT}_{\tilde{P}!}

shows that
\mathrm{CT}_{\tilde{P}^{-}\ast}
is
D(\mathrm{Bun}_{D},\Lambda)
-linear. In particular,
\mathrm{Eis}_{\tilde{P}^{-}!}
has a
D(\mathrm{Bun}_{D},\Lambda)
-linear right adjoint, which then base changes along
D(\mathrm{Bun}_{D},\Lambda)\to D(\Lambda)
to give the right adjoint of
\mathrm{Eis}_{P^{-}!}=\mathrm{Eis}_{\tilde{P}^{-}!}\otimes_{D(\mathrm{Bun}_{D},\Lambda)}D(\Lambda)
. This gives the isomorphism
\mathrm{CT}_{P^{-}\ast}\cong\mathrm{CT}_{P!}
.
∎

4.
The geometry of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Bun}}_{P}

In the last section we showed that the geometric Eisenstein functor
\mathrm{Eis}_{P!}(-)
preserves compact objects, establishing the first group of finiteness results claimed in Theorem
1.2.1
. To make further progress, we will now need to address how the functor interacts with the ULA objects. This will be accomplished by using the geometry of a certain relative compactification of the map
\mathfrak{p}_{P}
known in the classical geometric Langlands program as the Drinfeld compactification. In this section, we will establish some of the fundamental geometric properties of these compactifications.

The definition of the Drinfeld compactifications classically involves certain injections of vector bundles whose cokernel is flat in a suitable sense, where many of its basic properties in turn follow from the classical theory of Quot schemes. We now study the analogue of this in the Fargues-Fontaine setting. Several of the results can also be found in
[
Ham22
, § 5.2.3]
, but we recall the proofs here for convenience.

4.1.
Flat coherent sheaves on relative Fargues-Fontaine curves

Let
S\in\mathrm{Perf}
, with
X_{S}
the associated relative curve. If
\mathcal{E}
is an
\mathcal{O}_{X_{S}}
-module and
T\to S
is any map of perfectoid spaces, we write
\mathcal{E}_{T}
for the pullback of
\mathcal{E}
along the map of ringed spaces
X_{T}\to X_{S}
. The following key notion is due to Anschütz-le Bras
[
ALB21
, Definition 2.8]
.

Definition 4.1.1
.

A (relatively) flat coherent sheaf on
X_{S}
is an
\mathcal{O}_{X_{S}}
-module
\mathcal{E}
which, locally in the analytic topology on
S
, can be written as the cokernel of a fiberwise-injective map of bundles
\mathcal{E}_{1}\to\mathcal{E}_{2}
.

For some explanation of the name, see
[
ALB21
, Remark 2.9]
.

We next record some foundational stability properties of this notion. Part (3) of the next proposition is
[
ALB21
, Theorem 2.11]
. Part (2) is
[
Ham22
, Lemma 5.14]
.

Proposition 4.1.2
.

(1)

If
\mathcal{F}
is a flat coherent sheaf on
X_{S}
and
f:\mathcal{E}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{F}
is any surjection from a vector bundle, then
\ker f
is a vector bundle and the natural map
\ker f\to\mathcal{E}
is fiberwise injective.

(2)

Flat coherent sheaves are stable under base change on
S
, kernels of surjections, cokernels of fiberwise-injective maps, and tensoring by vector bundles.

(3)

The functor sending
S
to the groupoid of flat coherent sheaves on
X_{S}
is a stack for the v-topology.

Proof.

(1) is local on
|X_{S}|
, where it reduces to the following standard fact from commutative algebra: if
f:A^{n}\twoheadrightarrow M
is any surjection from a finite free
A
-module onto a finitely presented module of projective dimension
\leq 1
